- **2026-09-05 — the field list carries three keys, not two (ruled 2026-09-05).** A9.2 names `S_pc`/`PK_pc`; this party's own `PK_pc` joins them, for the reason A4.8's note of the same date gives — an ordinary channel frame binds the sealer's own verifying key, and ML-DSA-87 cannot derive it from the signing half. It is a fixed-width field of the same single write and changes nothing about the atomicity argument above. Build note: `dm::resume::ResumeRecord`.

### A9.3 — the decision framing corrected, and both endpoints priced (closes metadata CM-A8-1, CM-A8-2, CM-A8-3)

A8.3 inverted the sign of the tradeoff. A9 corrects it, and the final stress round sharpened the basis: **dropping reconnection does not remove the onset beacon — it relocates the onset to the doorbell, which is addressed by a seed world-derivable from the recipient's harvested pubkey (`:68`), so its onset is visible to a strictly LARGER observer set than the channel's.** The load-bearing truth is **observer-set size**: the channel `RE-EST` group is `ss`-derived/pseudonymous, its address not third-party-computable, so its onset is visible only to an incumbent co-host of one opaque record; the doorbell onset is visible to anyone who harvests the recipient's pubkey, no incumbency needed. (Precise basis, per the metadata review: a doorbell co-host learns the **recipient vertex + knock event + in-degree**, *not* the sender↔recipient **edge** — the clear-edge leak belongs to F7's separate invite-only lobby-grant path, `:293`; the earlier "graph-edge in the clear" wording is corrected here.) So the true choice is **channel-onset (a `RE-EST` group, visible to one incumbent co-host, no new graph vertex exposed) versus doorbell-onset (visible to a world-derivable-address observer set)** — and reconnection is metadata equal-or-*better*. A9 therefore **recommends accepting reconnection**, and reframes the `C` call as a downtime-*resolution* / availability / write-budget tuning, not an accept-a-leak decision. The one inversion, named for completeness: under `ss`-compromise / harvest-now-decrypt-later (an admitted live property), the persistent channel `RE-EST` is post-hoc recoverable while a decayed doorbell onset is gone — but this corner is strictly dominated, because under `ss`-compromise the message content leaks regardless, so the onset is marginal. Equal-or-better holds in the live-observer model that governs the decision.

Two pricing completions fold in here: the residual samples **both** endpoints — the onset `RE-EST` timestamps the reconnecting party and the `RE-ACK` timestamps the answering party's liveness (a second F1 presence sample) — and **A8.4's promptness bound is an answer-side onset amplifier** (a bounded-latency `RE-ACK` couples tightly to the answerer's online-transition, and the matched `RE-EST`→`RE-ACK` pair pins both endpoints); this promptness↔crispness coupling is a genuine availability↔metadata tension, accepted as an obligation rather than fixable. And A9.1's fresh-re-encap option (b), if a build takes it, shows a co-host two `RE-EST` writes for one logical attempt after a crash — a rare "crashed-mid-re-establishment" micro-signal that inflates the downtime readout; named here, folded into the onset residual (and avoided entirely by A9.1's default (a)).

### A9.4 — the last doc-scope tightenings (closes crypto F-A8-1, F-A8-4)

Two ordering facts A8 left implicit: **(i)** the establish→delete transition (the provisional/resume record is deleted on establishment, not atomic with it, #279) gets its precedence rule — establishment commits before steady-state, and a crash in the window re-emits a stale-openable `RE-EST` at an already-established peer, which the confirmation-locked supersede resolves recovering-loud; the divergence table gains the row `(we-crashed-post-establish, peer-established) → supersede → recovering`. **(ii)** In the two-rate split (A8.4), header/`attempt` dedup is evaluated **before** the response-emission budget is charged, so a crash-loop re-emitting the same attempt (A9.1) is deduped and never drains the peer's `RE-ACK` budget — the property that makes A8.4's DoS-resistance hold.

### The divergence table, A9 deltas

A3.15's table, as amended through A5, stands. A9.4(i) adds one state pair, which the establish→delete transition makes reachable and no earlier row covers:

| # | State pair | What happens | Outcome |
|---|---|---|---|
| 14 | We crashed after establishment committed but before the resume record was deleted; the peer is already established | the provisional/resume record is deleted on establishment, not atomically with it (#279), so a crash in that window re-emits a stale-openable `RE-EST` at an already-established peer; the confirmation-locked supersede resolves it | recovering-loud |

The transition's precedence rule is **establishment commits before steady-state**. A9.4(i) states the rule and this outcome and offers no further justification; none is supplied here either, so the row carries what the amendment ratifies and nothing more. No row is silent or terminal-and-silent.

### On ratification

The ISC family gains criteria for A9.1–A9.4, superseding the A8 criteria where revised. This is the loop's cap amendment; six rounds have converged the architecture, and the A8 round's three axes called A9 ratifiable. **The two human items that remain, and only these:** (1) **the feature-level call, now correctly signed (A9.3)** — accept reconnection (the metadata-preferable option — A9's recommendation) versus require fresh first contact after every teardown (worse: doorbell onset visible to a larger world-derivable-address observer set); and if accepted, (2) **the `C` downtime-resolution/availability tuning**, defaulted to a middle setting, with the absolute leaked-integer numbers to be closed at the byte pass. **The build-obligation list** (the durable `replace_atomically`; the single-blob field set of A9.2; the re-emit invariant of A9.1; the `(gen,seq)` scoping; the `flock`; reuse-not-skip on the re-establishment step; **A9.4's two ordering facts — the establish→delete precedence rule behind row 14, and `attempt` dedup evaluated before the response-emission budget is charged**) is the implementer's contract. The two ordering facts were stated in A9.4 and omitted from this list, which left a ratified property outside the implementer's contract; they are listed here without change to what A9.4 says. #262 closes citing A9. Until ratification, #262 stays open and this section binds nothing.

**Status: FROZEN 2026-07-28 (ratified).** DRAFT v6 is the design-of-record. The ISC-C38–C46 / A-C20–A-C25 family in `ISA.md` is re-cut to v6, the build-slice issues are open (#177 forced), and this doc is the frozen reference for the build. Any change now is a deliberate, reviewed amendment — not a redraft.

**Channel re-establishment: RATIFIED at Amendment A9 (2026-07-30).** The A1→A9 amendment chain designs channel re-establishment after a restart; A9 is its design-of-record. Reconnection is accepted (metadata-preferable to the doorbell fallback); `C` is accepted as a tunable middle-default dial. The ISC family gains criteria for A9.1–A9.4 (superseding earlier amendments' criteria where revised) at the build-slice cut. #262 is answered and closed citing A9. Amendments A1–A8 above are superseded and retained only as the refutation trail. Lands via PR #284.

## Decision — what names a correspondence directory on disk (ratified; closes #288)

The A1–A9 chain settles what a correspondence *stores* and never settles what its directory is *called*. This decision fixes it. It is not an amendment — A9's mechanisms are untouched — but it is a boundary, so it is recorded here with the rest of the re-establishment design.

**The decision: a random per-correspondence label, minted from the CSPRNG at first contact and stored in the contact cache.** 32 bytes, hex-encoded as the directory name. `storage::dm_store` already takes an opaque `CorrespondenceLabel` and depends on no property of it beyond distinctness, so nothing in the store changes.

### What is actually keyless, which is more than the name

Two claims about this layout do not survive inspection: that the directory name is "the one part of the record layout readable without any key", and that the correspondent count is the only residual. **Both are wrong**, and the corrected picture is the honest input to the decision:

- **`cursor.bin` is plaintext — ⚠️ SUPERSEDED/STRUCK: the receive cursor is sealed at rest.** ~~`ReceiveCursor` is the one unsealed `RecordKind`; its eight bytes are a big-endian page number, so an observer with no key reads a monotone proxy for received volume per correspondence.~~ **There is no unsealed `RecordKind`. The cursor is sealed like every other kind, so a keyless observer reads its presence and its bucketed size, never the page number, and the monotone received-volume proxy this bullet named is gone. The two bullets below are unaffected and remain the keyless surface.**
- **Record *presence* reveals handshake state.** The four filenames are fixed, so `provisional.bin` present means an unconfirmed handshake and `resume.bin` means an established one.
- **mtimes are never normalized**, so each directory carries a timestamp series.

Two disk snapshots therefore yield a per-directory cursor-delta and activity timeline that joins to a candidate correspondent's observed online windows — which is A9's F1 cross-plane join, already priced and accepted. **A name cannot fix any of this.** What a name can decide is whether the directory is linkable to an identity *without observation*, from a listing alone, and that is the question this decision answers.

### Why not the two derived options

**A hash of the recipient's key-record address** is ruled out outright. The address is world-derivable from a harvested public key, so anyone who can read the profile directory and holds a candidate pubkey can test membership over any candidate set, from a single listing, with no observation and no key. That is a strictly stronger attack than the behavioural join above, and it is the one a name can prevent.

**Salting that derivation with a stored per-profile secret** is rejected — but on a narrower argument than the obvious one. The obvious argument, that a salt falls to the later key compromise of #293 while a random label "has no preimage to surrender", does not survive contact with the layout: `DmStore::open` takes the profile at-rest key, **the same material that protects the contact cache**, so the compromise that surrenders a salt also surrenders the cache — a direct label→identity table needing no candidate set at all. Under key compromise the two options are equivalent, and if anything the cache is the more informative artefact.

The differential that does hold is against the attacker with the **disk but not the key**, and it is about *lifetime*:

> A salt is a standing oracle. It answers "is this candidate pubkey a correspondent?" for every directory it ever named — **including orphaned directories whose contact-cache entry was deleted long ago** — because the salt must outlive every correspondence in order to name any of them. A minted label reveals only what the cache still holds, so deleting a contact deletes its linkage.

That is a real and permanent asymmetry, and it is the reason for the decision. It is also partly self-undercut and the doc should say so: #293 records that erasure is `unlink` rather than overwrite, so a deleted cache row may itself be recoverable from unallocated blocks. The asymmetry is therefore strong against a live-filesystem read and weaker against forensic recovery — still favouring the minted label, but not absolutely.

### The obligation this places on the collection slice (#236)

A random label cannot be recomputed, so **there is no recovering from a half-completed first contact.** Minting the label, writing the contact-cache entry, and creating the directory must be ordered so a crash at any point leaves either nothing or something a sweep can identify and remove — the same discipline A9 applies as commit-then-emit, and the same family as the orphaned `.tmp.*` siblings of #286. A directory with no cache entry naming it is unreachable forever and must be sweepable; a cache entry naming a directory that was never created must be re-mintable.

Note also that `CorrespondenceLabel::from_bytes` remains public and unconstrained, so nothing in the type system prevents #236 from writing `from_bytes(hash(address))` and reintroducing exactly the forbidden state. ISC-A-C44 is the contract; there is no mechanical enforcement of it, and the collection slice's review is where that gets checked.

### Accepted residual: correspondent count

**No naming scheme conceals cardinality.** Anyone with read access counts the directories. Hiding the count would need cover directories indistinguishable from real ones — and indistinguishable now means forged mtimes, not merely a plausible name. It would also not work: each correspondence occupies fixed buckets totalling roughly 328 KB, so **total profile size reports the correspondent count even if every name were hidden**. (Justifying this by write rhythm instead is weaker than it sounds: a dormant correspondence emits no rhythm but still occupies a directory. The size argument is the one that holds.) (A cover directory does not additionally have to forge a plausibly-evolving plaintext cursor — the cursor is sealed at rest. The size argument is untouched by that.) Accepted, and recorded as accepted.

## Decision — scrub every record before unlinking it (ratified; closes #293)

`Locked::delete` unlinked a record and fsynced the directory. The name went away; the blocks did not. An adversary who reads unallocated blocks and **later** obtains the profile key recovers the sealed record and opens it — for the provisional record that is `ss0`, which roots `RK0` and reopens the early chain. That is precisely the harvest-now-decrypt-later exposure the erasure was introduced to bound, so the property was weakened rather than absent.

**The decision: overwrite the record in two fsynced phases, then unlink. Every record kind, not only the provisional one.**

### Why the delete path and not the replace path

A7 named the tension and A9 accepted it: crash-safe replacement and real erasure are opposed, because tmp-plus-rename leaves the superseded blob's blocks intact and merely unlinked. That trade stands for `replace_atomically` and is untouched here.

The *delete* path is different, and the difference is what makes this affordable. Replacement must be crash-atomic because a torn replacement leaves a record that is neither the old value nor the new one. Deletion has no such requirement: the postcondition is "this record does not exist", and `TeardownCause` already treats an absent record and an unusable one identically — *"the outcome and the remedy are the same"*. Losing a record mid-delete costs nothing that losing it any other way does not already cost.

### Why every kind, not just the provisional record

#293 was raised about `ss0`. But `Locked::delete` is generic over `RecordKind`, and the resume record holds A9.2's complete field set — keys, generation, sequence numbers — under exactly the same later-compromise threat. Scrubbing only the record that prompted the issue would have left the larger secret behind. Scoped to the general delete, so a kind added later is covered without anyone remembering to.

### The two phases, and why they are two

An erasure sentinel is written and **fsynced first**; the body is overwritten and fsynced second; only then is the name unlinked and the directory fsynced.

The split buys a distinguishable crash window. Collapsed into one write, a torn scrub leaves a record that fails to open — byte-indistinguishable from truncation or tampering, so a power cut would surface to the user as a possible attack. With the sentinel durable first, any crash from that point leaves a record a reader can *name*: `DmStoreError::ErasureInterrupted`, whose own variant exists so a deletion cut short is never reported as tampering. A user told "possible tampering" once too often stops believing it when it is true.

**The barrier between the scrub and the unlink is load-bearing, not hygiene.** Without it the overwrite may still be dirty page cache when the name goes away, and a filesystem is free to never write those blocks at all — a no-op that looks exactly like a fix, and that no test of the resulting *bytes* could tell apart. It is asserted by barrier count, not by inspection.

### The ceiling, stated rather than implied

On an SSD the FTL remaps an overwrite to a fresh erase block; a copy-on-write filesystem writes a new extent by design. In both cases the original blocks survive untouched and this buys nothing. It buys real erasure on ext4-over-LUKS on rotating or dm-mapped storage, which is the ordinary deployment, and it is best-effort by construction everywhere else. The code says so and no caller should read it as a guarantee. Filesystem-level secure deletion is not portable and not something the application can assert on the user's behalf.

### What this firms up elsewhere

The #288 decision (a minted, non-derived correspondence directory name) rests its surviving argument on deletion: *one erased contact-cache row kills that correspondent's linkage forever*. That argument was explicitly recorded as partly self-undercut, because an unlinked cache row may itself be recoverable from unallocated blocks. Scrubbing on delete narrows that gap — not to zero, subject to the ceiling above, but the two decisions now point the same way instead of one quietly weakening the other.

### What it costs: `establish` is no longer a safe retry (ratified)

`PendingHandshake::establish` builds the ratchet and then deletes the provisional record, in that order, so that a failed deletion leaves the record on disk and the whole call can be retried. **The scrub ends that.** A crash after the sentinel's barrier leaves a record that no longer opens, so a handshake a pre-scrub crash would have left resumable is destroyed.

Accepted, on three grounds.

The trade is **forced by the design**. The steady-state ratchet is deliberately never persisted — that is what makes erasing `ss0` worth anything — so there is nothing to durably commit before the delete, and no ordering survives the crash without persisting exactly what the design refuses.

The two losses **differ in kind**. A destroyed provisional record costs the availability of one in-progress handshake, recoverable by redoing first contact. Leaving the blocks intact costs the confidentiality of `ss0` permanently, against a later key compromise. Under this threat model availability yields to confidentiality.

The **frequencies are asymmetric**. The scrub protects every establishment; this window is two fsyncs on one small file inside a single critical section.

The failure stays legible. An interrupted erase surfaces as `ErasureInterrupted`, and `restart_channel` maps it to `TeardownCause::NoProvisionalRecord` — the record is *lost*, not the store *unreadable*. The distinction is behavioural: `StoreUnreadable` promises nothing was lost and makes the outbox retain queued messages, which would leave the caller waiting on a handshake that cannot come back.

### The sweep scrubs too, and fails soft where `delete` fails hard

A crashed `replace_atomically` leaves a temp sibling that may hold a whole sealed record, so the startup sweep scrubs before unlinking — otherwise ciphertext leaves by a path that never passes through `delete`.

**A sibling that cannot be scrubbed is skipped, not fatal.** The sweep runs inside `DmStore::open`, so returning an error there makes the store unopenable for every correspondence — a mode-0444 sibling, or a directory whose name matches the temp infix, would brick it permanently. Skipping leaves the file exactly where it already was, unscrubbed, which is the state before the sweep existed. Unlinking it anyway is the one option worse than both: it launders the ciphertext out of reach while reporting success.

`Locked::delete` keeps the opposite disposition and fails closed, because there the postcondition *is* the erasure.

- **2026-08-14 — send `seq` does not restart at a new generation, and A9.2's parenthetical saying otherwise is struck (ratified; closes #313).** A9.2 justified qualifying the send-side floor by generation *"so a committed re-establishment root (a new generation, under which send `seq` legitimately restarts at 0) does not trip a false rollback alarm."* The premise is false against the build and against this document's own frame. `Ratchet::step_send` starts a new generation's chain at the current `next_send_seq` and never resets it, so send `seq` is monotone per direction for the life of the conversation — and A9's own survivor set, three paragraphs above A9.2, lists **counter-continuity crypto** among the items A9 does not relitigate. The parenthetical contradicted the section it sat in. **Three independent surfaces make continuity load-bearing rather than incidental**, none of them namespaced by generation: `seq` *is* the DHT address (`position_of` divides it into page and slot, and the page owner seed descends from `AR`, which re-establishment does not re-root), it is the outbox's only key (one direction's outbox spans the whole correspondence from the doorbell knock at sequence 0 onward, and carries no generation), and it is the acknowledgement's position — a monotone contiguous prefix whose ceiling is the highest sequence actually sent. That last one is decisive on its own: against a pre-existing prefix, a restarted `seq` is indistinguishable from a replay, so the sender would read *settled* for messages that were never collected, which is the false-*delivered* § D-DELIV exists to forbid. The frozen v6 anti-replay rule is `chan_id ‖ seq` monotonic — epoch was dropped from `msg_sig` at v2 (§ MAJOR c4, *"epoch is addressing-only"*), so nothing else disambiguates two messages sharing a sequence number. **What the generation is actually for, and what the amended text now says:** with a bare counter, *"re-established and nothing sent yet"* and *"a replayed stale resume blob"* are the same value; the pair tells them apart. The qualification is retained on that ground. **No code changes** — the build was already correct; it was the justification that was wrong, which is the more dangerous of the two, because a reader checking the stated reason finds it false and concludes the field is unnecessary. Build note: `dm::resume::SendFloor` carries this reasoning at the type.

- **2026-09-04 — `chan_id_{n+1}` is a sibling of the re-established root, under a new label `DM_REESTAB_CHAN_ID`, whose byte value is `daemonseed/dm/reest/chanid/v1`.** The frozen text derives no successor channel identifier and needs one. § D-PFS deletes `chan_id` at establishment and the retained set at `:710` does not include it; § v4 at `:670` puts `chan_id` outside every at-rest encoding; A9.2's re-establishment blob does not carry it; and the only mention of a new identifier — A8.2's rollback scoping at `:1319` — names the value without saying where it comes from. So a resumed channel would have had no identifier to bind into `msg_sig` or the seal AAD, and no frame could be written under it. The derivation is a **second output of the extraction Clause 1 already performs**: `chan_id_{n+1} = HKDF-Expand(HKDF-Extract(DM_REESTAB_SALT, RS_n ‖ ss_new), DM_REESTAB_CHAN_ID)`, at `ROOT_LEN`, produced in the same call as `RS_{n+1}` so the two cannot be spelled apart. Both parties reach it from material they both hold, exactly as they reach `RS_{n+1}`. `RK_0'` is **not** a sibling of these two: `:722` derives it through `advance_root`, a separate extraction salted with `RS_n` itself.

  **Derived rather than retained, and that is the whole of the choice.** The alternative was to keep `chan_id_n` at rest beside `AR` and carry it across the re-establishment. That amends the establishment-deletion rule and the retained set at `:710`, contradicts `:670`'s "`chan_id` and `AR` are not in the encoding at all", and changes what A8.2's rollback high-water is scoped by — for no property `AR` at rest does not already provide, since a party that holds `AR` can already address the channel. Deriving it keeps the at-rest set to `AR` and `RS_n` and leaves both rules intact.

  **The accepted cost is a bounded retention on the answering side.** The responder holds `chan_id_n` in memory beside the superseded receive-side ratchet state, because frames already published under the old identifier must still open while the dead direction drains. A3.12 already retains that receive state on the same terms and bounds it by the give-up, so the identifier is retained for no longer than the state it belongs to, and a restart during the drain loses both — the ordinary restart cost, not a new one.

  **A sibling of the successor root, not a chain off it**, for the reason every fan-out here is: `RS_{n+1}` is written to disk and `chan_id_{n+1}` is bound into every signature the resumed channel writes, so a chain in either direction would let one of them yield the other. The two share an extraction and differ only by label, which is what the pinned vectors in `dm::resume` check — a build that expanded the identifier under the successor's label would collide them, and the vector says so.

  **Derived but not yet consumed.** The value is produced and returned; nothing reads it, because the resumed-channel send path is unbuilt and the established-channel path binds the identifier derived at first contact. The obligation this note records is that the resumed path bind *this* value when it is built.

  **A8.2's numbering half is untouched by the above and stands unamended.** `:1319`'s *"a legitimate re-establishment restarts generation numbering under a new `chan_id`"* is superseded on the numbering clause by A3.9 at `:917` and by the 2026-08-14 note at `:1479`, both of which have the clear generation counter continue rather than restart; only the "new `chan_id`" half is what this note supplies a derivation for.
